## Reasoning

**Understanding the mission:**
- Before switch time: robots should MOVE (score +1 per stationary robot)
- After switch time: robots should STOP (score +1 per moving robot)
- Switch signal: a wall block lights up GREEN
- Floor is uniformly GRAY throughout

**Target behavior:**
- Default: MOVE (exploration/random walk)
- When GREEN is perceived: STOP

**Key perceptions available:**
- C=7: perceives a color with camera → use to detect GREEN (color 2)
- C=5: fixed probability → for fallback transitions
- C=3/C=4: neighbor counts

**State design:**
- State 0: Exploration (moving) → transition to Stop when GREEN seen (C=7, l=2)
- State 1: Stop → transition back to Exploration if GREEN not perceived (C=5 with low prob, or C=4 with 0 neighbors)

The key insight: robots should move by default, then stop when they see green. They should stay stopped as long as green is visible, and maybe return to moving if green disappears (but since the wall stays lit after switch, they should stay stopped).

Let me design 14 machines:

1. **Best candidate**: 2-state: Explore→Stop on green, Stop→Explore on low prob (to handle case where green disappears from view)
2. Variants with different probabilities for the transitions
3. 3-state variants for richer behavior
4. Variants using attraction/repulsion

For the green color: color id 2 = green.

Let me create the population:

**Machine 1 (baseline):** State0=Explore, State1=Stop. From S0: see green→S1. From S1: low prob→S0 (so they can re-evaluate).

**Machine 2:** Same but with higher probability of staying stopped (lower return prob).

**Machine 3:** 3-state with repulsion before stopping.

**Machine 4:** State0=Explore with many neighbors→Stop state. State1=Stop when green seen.

Let me write them out carefully:

This means: S0=Explore, if see green go to S1=Stop. S1=Stop, with prob 0.01 go back to S0 (very rarely restart moving).

**Design philosophy:**
- Core 2-state: S0=Explore, S1=Stop. S0 sees green → S1. S1 rarely → S0.
- The key is that once green is seen, robots stop. They should mostly stay stopped.
- Returning to S0 from S1 with very low probability is okay because S0 will quickly see green again and stop.

**For 3-state:**
- S0=Explore, S1=Stop, S2=GoToColor(green). 
- S0 sees green → S1 (stop directly)
- S0 random → S2 (go toward green to find it faster)
- S2 sees green → S1 (stop when arrived)
- S1 rarely → S0

Actually GoToColor makes robots move, which is good before switch. After switch, they should stop. Once any robot sees green and stops, others nearby might also detect green.

FSM: --nstates 4 --s0 0 --rwm0 10 --cle0 0 --n0 2 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --l0x0 2 --p0x0 1.0 --n0x1 1 --c0x1 5 --p0x1 0.01 --s1 1 --cle1 0 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.005 --s2 8 --vel2 1.0 --clr2 2 --cle2 0 --n2 2 --n2x0 2 --c2x0 7 --l2x0 2 --p2x0 1.0 --n2x1 0 --c2x1 5 --p2x1 0.01 --s3 0 --rwm3 50 --cle3 0 --n3 2 --n3x0 2 --c3x0 7 --l3x0 2 --p3x0 1.0 --n3x1 0 --c3x1 5 --p3x1 0.005

For 4-state: S0 others=[1,2,3]: D=0→S1(Stop) on green, D=1→S2(GoToColor) randomly. S1 others=[0,2,3]: D=0→S0 rarely. S2 others=[0,1,3]: D=2→S3? No D goes 0,1,2 for 3 other states. D=0→S0, D=1→S1, D=2→S3.

Wait: with 4 states, leaving state 2 the others are [0,1,3]. D=0→S0, D=1→S1, D=2→S3.
So n2x0 D=2→S3... that means destination 2 points to S3. 
And n2x1 D=0→S0.

Let me re-examine: "With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2."

So for N states, leaving state I, the others are all states except I, in order. D=0 is the first, D=1 the second, etc.

For 4 states, leaving state 2: others are [0,1,3]. D=0→S0, D=1→S1, D=2→S3.

So in my 4-state machine:
- S2 n2x0: D=2→S3. n2x1: D=0→S0. 
- S3 n3x0: D=2→S1(Stop). For S3 others=[0,1,2], D=2→S2. Hmm.

This is getting complex. Let me just write the final 14 machines cleanly.

Let me count: I need exactly 14 machines.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I notice I haven't received any feedback about scores from the previous rounds. Let me reason from first principles and provide a strong population.

## Reasoning

**Mission analysis:**
- Before switch (~400-600 steps): robots should MOVE → minimize stationary robots
- After switch: robots should STOP → minimize moving robots
- Signal: a GREEN wall lights up at switch time
- Floor is always GRAY → floor color conditions are useless

**Optimal behavior:**
- Default state: MOVE (exploration)
- Trigger to STOP: perceive GREEN (C=7, l=2)
- Once stopped, stay stopped (very low return probability)

**Key design choices:**
- S0=Explore (moving), S1=Stop
- S0→S1: C=7 (see green, l=2, p=1.0)
- S1→S0: C=5 (tiny prob, ~0.002-0.01) — necessary since must have transition, but should be rare
- Varying rwm (turning rate) for exploration coverage
- Some 3-state variants with GoToColor(green) to help robots find the green wall faster

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I still haven't received score feedback. Let me continue with strong designs based on mission analysis, while diversifying the population further.

## Reasoning

**Core logic recap:**
- Before switch: MOVE (exploration) → minimize stationary penalty
- After switch: STOP when GREEN wall appears → minimize moving penalty
- GREEN detection via C=7, l=2 (color 2 = green)

**Key insight I want to explore more:** The GoToColor(green) behavior makes robots actively seek the green wall. This could be very powerful: before the switch, robots move anyway (GoToColor moves them); after switch, they see green up close and can stop. But GoToColor might cluster robots near one wall, reducing coverage.

**New ideas to explore:**
1. Start in GoToColor(green) by default — robots always move toward any green they see, explore otherwise
2. Use neighbor-based signaling: once some robots stop (having seen green), others nearby might infer the switch happened
3. 3-state: Explore → GoToColor(green) → Stop, with green perception triggers
4. Vary the return probability from Stop more carefully

**Population strategy:**
- 4 machines: best 2-state core variants (elitism + small tweaks)
- 4 machines: 3-state with GoToColor intermediate
- 3 machines: neighbor-signaling variants (stopped robots have 0 neighbors if others leave, or many if clustered)
- 3 machines: exploration variants (different behaviors in moving state)

For 3-state machines, destination indexing reminder:
- State 0, others=[1,2]: D=0→S1, D=1→S2
- State 1, others=[0,2]: D=0→S0, D=1→S2
- State 2, others=[0,1]: D=0→S0, D=1→S1

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I still have no score feedback. Let me reflect more carefully on what might be going wrong and diversify more aggressively.

## Reasoning

**Re-examining the mission more carefully:**

The score formula: before switch → +1 per stationary robot per step; after switch → +1 per moving robot per step.

With 20 robots and ~500 steps before switch + ~700 steps after:
- If robots always move: score ≈ 0 (before) + 20×700 = 14000 (after)
- If robots always stop: score ≈ 20×500 + 0 = 10000 (before)
- Optimal: move before, stop after = 0 + 0 = 0 theoretically

**The GREEN signal is the key.** Robots that see the green wall should stop. The problem is that not all robots will be facing/near the green wall at switch time.

**What if GREEN detection isn't reliable?** Maybe I should also consider:
- Using neighbor count signals: robots that stopped (having seen green) become stationary, creating a "cluster" signal
- If stopped robots have many neighbors, others could detect "many neighbors" → also stop

**New approach: neighbor-cascade stopping**
- S0=Explore → see green → S1=Stop
- S0=Explore → many neighbors (stopped cluster) → S1=Stop
- S1=Stop → very rarely → S0

**Also considering:** Maybe the issue is that GoToColor(green) is counterproductive after switch — robots keep moving toward the green wall instead of stopping when they reach it. The C=7 condition should catch them.

**Also trying:** 1-state machines (always stop or always move won't work). Pure Stop is bad before switch. Pure Explore is bad after.

Let me try much more aggressive neighbor-cascade and also simpler/cleaner designs:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I still have no score feedback, which is unusual. Let me reconsider my approach more fundamentally.

## Reasoning

**Critical re-examination:**

I've been assuming GREEN = color index 2. Let me verify: the color list is {1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan}. Yes, green = 2. That seems right.

**What might be failing?**

1. The C=7 condition fires when the robot "perceives a given colour with its camera" - this detects colored *objects* in view. The green wall block should be detectable by the camera. This should work.

2. Maybe the issue is that robots need to be *facing* the green wall to detect it. With random exploration, not all robots will face the green wall in time.

3. Maybe I should try a completely different approach: **use a timer-like mechanism** with random probability to switch from moving to stopping, calibrated to the switch time distribution (400-600 steps = center ~500).

**Alternative approach: probabilistic timer**
- Move for a while, then switch to stop based on accumulated probability
- C=5 with p=0.002 per step → expected wait = 500 steps → good match!
- This doesn't need green detection at all

**Pure timer approach:**
- S0=Explore → C=5 p=0.002 → S1=Stop (expected 500 steps, matching switch time)
- S1=Stop → very rarely back (p=0.001)

**Hybrid approach:**
- S0=Explore → green seen OR timer → S1=Stop
- Timer: p=0.002 gives geometric distribution centered at 500 steps

**Why timer might work:** The switch time is uniform[400,600], mean=500. If each robot independently switches at step ~500 (via p≈0.002/step), they'll be moving before and stopped after on average.

Let me now generate a diverse population mixing GREEN-detection, timer-based, and hybrid approaches:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I still have no score feedback. This is very concerning - it suggests either all my machines are performing similarly (so no clear winner emerges) or there's a systematic issue. Let me think much more carefully.

## Deep Reasoning

**Re-reading the scoring rule:**
- Before switch: +1 per STATIONARY robot per step → robots should MOVE
- After switch: +1 per MOVING robot per step → robots should STOP

**The theoretical optimum:** Score = 0 if robots move perfectly before and stop perfectly after.

**What score would "always move" get?** With switch at ~500, after-switch duration ~700 steps: 20×700 = 14000.
**What score would "always stop" get?** Before-switch ~500 steps: 20×500 = 10000.
**Mixed optimal:** ~0 if perfect.

**The timer approach (p=0.002/step) analysis:**
- Expected time to fire: 1/0.002 = 500 steps ✓
- But it's geometric distribution, so variance is high
- Some robots will stop at step 100, others at step 900
- Average score contribution from premature stopping + late moving

**Actually the timer approach might be WORSE than always-move or always-stop!** Let me calculate:

For timer with p per step, robot stops at geometric(p) step T_stop:
- Cost before switch (T_switch): if T_stop < T_switch, robot is stationary for (T_switch - T_stop) steps before switch
- Cost after switch: if T_stop > T_switch, robot is moving for (T_stop - T_switch) steps after switch

With p=0.002, T_switch~U[400,600]:
- Many robots will stop before the switch (adding pre-switch cost)
- Many robots won't stop until well after switch

**The GREEN detection is fundamentally better** because it's triggered by the actual event. The issue must be something else.

**New hypothesis:** Maybe not all robots can see the green wall. The green wall is ONE block, on ONE side. Robots far from it or facing away won't see it. Once some robots stop, they no longer block others' view - but robots spread around the arena may never see the green wall.

**Solution: neighbor cascade.** When some robots stop (having seen green), they cluster near the wall. Other robots should detect "many stationary neighbors" and also stop. But C=3/C=4 count ALL neighbors within range, including moving ones.

**Better solution:** GoToColor(green) to spread awareness. If robots actively move toward green, they'll all eventually see it.

But wait - GoToColor makes robots MOVE after the switch, which costs points! Unless they stop when they actually see it close up.

**Actually the best strategy might be:**
- Move randomly before switch (score=0)
- When green appears, ALL robots need to stop quickly
- Robots near the green wall stop immediately
- Other robots need a cascade mechanism

**Cascade via LED colors:** Stopped robots could emit a specific LED color, and others detect it via C=7. Let me try:
- S0=Explore (cle=0, off)
- S1=Stop (cle=4, yellow) — stopped robots emit yellow
- S0→S1: see green (C=7, l=2) OR see yellow neighbor (C=7, l=4... but wait, C=7 uses camera to see colored OBJECTS, not LED colors of neighbors)

Hmm, C=7 detects colored objects in the environment (walls, floor markers), not robot LEDs. So this doesn't work directly.

**Range-and-bearing:** The neighbor count conditions (C=3, C=4) count robots within 0.5m range. This is limited.

**Actually, let me reconsider the whole problem.** Maybe a simple design that works:

The key realization: **before switch, score is minimized by moving. After switch, by stopping.** The green light is the ONLY signal. If robots can't all see it, some will keep moving.

**What if I use GoToColor(green) as the primary state?** GoToColor makes robots move toward green if they see it, and... what if they don't see it? They probably just go straight or turn. Either way they're moving before switch (good). After switch when green appears, they move TOWARD it, get close, see it strongly, and then... they need to transition to Stop.

The transition from GoToColor→Stop when seeing green (C=7, l=2, p=1.0) would fire immediately since they're already oriented toward green. This might work better than random exploration because robots actively seek the green wall.

**Let me try a completely different population focused on these insights:**

1. GoToColor-primary with green detection → Stop
2. Timer-based (pure probabilistic, calibrated)  
3. Hybrid green+timer
4. Neighbor cascade variants

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
Still no score feedback. Let me take a completely fresh perspective and think about what could be systematically wrong.

## Deep Re-examination

**What if my FSM syntax has been wrong all along?**

Let me re-read the rules very carefully:

- `--n<I>x<K> D`: destination, indexed among the OTHER states only. With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.

So for 2-state machines: ALL destinations must be D=0 (the only other state). I've been writing `--n0x0 0` (correct) and `--n1x0 0` (correct). That seems fine.

**What about the `--n<I> T` parameter?** It says "number of transitions leaving state I, integer in [1..4]". I've been declaring this correctly.

**Wait - let me recheck my 3-state machines more carefully.**

For state 1 in a 3-state machine: others are [0, 2]. D=0→state0, D=1→state2.
In machine: `--n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.001` → D=0 means go to state 0. ✓

For state 2 in a 3-state machine: others are [0, 1]. D=0→state0, D=1→state1.
`--n2x0 1 --c2x0 7 --l2x0 2 --p2x0 1.0` → D=1 means go to state 1 (Stop). ✓

**The syntax looks correct to me.**

**What if the problem is that I'm not getting feedback because the simulator IS running but the scoring system works differently than I think?**

Let me re-read: "the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary" before switch, and "1 for every robot that is moving" after switch.

**A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.**

The Stop behavior (B=1) makes robots completely still → counts as stationary (good after switch, bad before).
Exploration (B=0) makes robots move → counts as moving (good before switch, bad after).

**This all seems correct. My designs should work.**

**New hypothesis: Maybe the lack of feedback means ALL my designs are performing at the same level** - perhaps they're all roughly equivalent and the optimizer hasn't found differentiation. Or perhaps the system just doesn't show me scores until I ask or they're shown differently.

**Let me try radically different approaches to ensure variety:**

1. **Pure timer** (no green detection): p=0.002 gives geometric(500) switching time
2. **Aggressive green detection** with very high sensitivity
3. **Multi-robot coordination** via neighbor counts
4. **GoAwayColor** - move away from green? No, that's counterproductive.
5. **Repulsion-based movement** before switch (ensures spreading = more coverage = better green detection)

**Actually, let me reconsider the timer calculation more carefully:**

If p=0.002/step, P(stop before step 400) = 1-(1-0.002)^400 = 1-0.998^400 ≈ 1-0.449 = 0.551

So 55% of robots stop BEFORE the switch even happens at step 400 (earliest switch). That's terrible - they're stationary and getting penalized before the switch.

For the switch at step 500 (median): P(stop before 500) = 1-0.998^500 ≈ 1-0.368 = 0.632

**The timer approach is actually quite bad!** Too many robots stop early.

Better timer: p=0.001/step → E[stop] = 1000 steps. P(stop before 400) ≈ 33%. Still bad.

**What about p=0.0005?** E[stop]=2000. Most robots never stop. Bad.

**The timer approach fundamentally doesn't work well here** because the distribution is geometric (memoryless) and can't be well-calibrated to a uniform[400,600] switch.

**GREEN DETECTION IS THE RIGHT APPROACH.** The challenge is coverage - not all robots see the green wall.

**Key insight I've been missing:** The green wall is ONE wall block. The arena has walls on all sides. Robots spread throughout the arena. Some robots will be near the green wall and detect it. Others won't.

**Solution: Neighbor-count cascade.** When stopped robots cluster near the green wall, moving robots nearby (within 0.5m range) detect "many neighbors" (C=3) and also stop. This creates a wave of stopping.

Let me design machines that use BOTH green detection AND neighbor cascade:
- S0=Explore → see green (C=7,l=2) → S1=Stop
- S0=Explore → many neighbors (C=3, threshold=3-5) → S1=Stop  
- S1=Stop → very rarely → S0

This neighbor cascade could propagate the stop signal across the swarm!

**Final population design:**

# ROUND 9/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
Still no score feedback. I need to think about this very differently.

## Complete Rethink

**Why am I getting no feedback?** Either the system doesn't show scores in this format, or all machines score identically. Let me assume the system is working and I need to keep improving.

**Let me reconsider the fundamental problem from scratch:**

The scoring: before switch → penalize stationary; after switch → penalize moving.

**What is the theoretical lower bound?**
- Before switch (~500 steps avg): if ALL robots move, score contribution = 0
- After switch (~700 steps avg): if ALL robots stop, score contribution = 0
- Perfect score = 0

**The green wall is the only signal.** But consider: the range-and-bearing sensor has 0.5m range. The arena size isn't specified but with 20 robots starting near the center, the arena is probably a few meters wide. A robot might need to travel to the wall to see the green block.

**Critical question: Can robots see the green wall from far away?** The camera on e-pucks in ARGoS typically has a limited range for detecting colored blobs. If the detection range is short, robots need to be physically near the green wall.

**Alternative interpretation:** Maybe C=7 (perceive color) is quite reliable - the camera has good range for wall-mounted colored blocks. In that case, my green-detection designs should work.

**Let me try something radically different: what if I make robots STOP by default and only MOVE when they detect something indicating pre-switch?**

But there's no signal for "before switch" - only green appears AT switch time.

**What if I try: Stop first, then Move?**
- S0=Stop → after some time → S1=Explore → never stop again
- This is backwards from what I've been doing!
- Before switch: robots stop (bad, costs points)... no this doesn't work.

**What about: Move, then Stop after fixed time?**
Using C=5 with very low probability creates geometric distribution. But I showed this is bad.

**Actually wait** - what if the issue is that I need robots to stop IMMEDIATELY when green appears, and my designs DO work, but the optimizer just hasn't converged yet and I'm not seeing feedback because there's nothing to report yet?

**Let me try the most extreme/clean versions:**

**Design A: Pure green detection, minimal complexity**
- S0=Explore(rwm=10), one transition: see green → S1=Stop
- S1=Stop, one transition: tiny prob → S0 (necessary but minimized)
- p_return = 0.0001 (almost never return, essentially permanent stop)

**Design B: GoToColor as primary mover**
- S0=GoToColor(green): moves robots toward green wall actively
- When they see green (they're heading toward it), stop
- S0→S1=Stop on C=7,l=2

**Design C: Two-explorer with cascade**
- S0=Explore → see green → S1=Stop
- S0=Explore → many neighbors (stopped near wall) → S1=Stop
- High neighbor threshold to avoid false positives before switch

**Key parameter to vary:** The return probability from Stop. Lower = better after switch but robots never re-check. With p=0.0001, expected time in Stop = 10000 steps >> 1200 step run = effectively permanent.

Let me make p_return extremely small (0.0001) for the best designs:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
Still no score feedback after 9 rounds. This is the final round. Let me make my absolute best attempt.

## Final Reasoning

**The core problem:** Move before switch, stop after switch. Green wall appears at switch time. Robots must detect green and stop.

**What I'm most confident about:**
1. S0=Explore → C=7 (see green, l=2, p=1.0) → S1=Stop is the right core mechanism
2. S1=Stop should almost never return (p=0.0001 makes it essentially permanent for a 1200-step run)
3. Neighbor cascade (C=3, many neighbors → stop) can help propagate the stop signal
4. GoToColor(green) as alternative mover keeps robots heading toward the green wall

**What I haven't tried:** Making the return probability from Stop literally 0... but the rules require at least 1 transition. With p=0.0001, expected wait = 10000 steps >> 1200 steps, so effectively permanent.

**Critical coverage problem:** With 20 robots in an arena, only a fraction face/see the green wall immediately. The neighbor cascade is important. Let me tune it carefully:
- Threshold of 3-4 neighbors with high steepness (w=15-20) = fire reliably when 4+ neighbors present
- Before switch: robots are spread out, few have 4+ neighbors → cascade doesn't fire prematurely
- After switch: stopped robots cluster near green wall, creating dense groups → cascade fires

**Best final population - focused and clean:**

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
